New Delhi, the 10th February 1971 

S.O. 1102 .—In exercise of the powers conferred by the proviso to .irtlcle 
309 of the Constitution, the President hereby makes the following rules, regu- 
ating the. method of recruitment to the posts of Assistant Store Keeper and 
Store Attendant In the National Fire Service College, Nagpur, namely:— 

1 . ( 1 ) Short title and commencement—These rules may be called the Assis¬ 
tant Store Keeper und Store attendant (National Fire Service College. Nagpur) 
x^ecruitment Kuies, itVl. 





Sec. 3 <ii)I THE GAZETTE (MF IWIMA: JiHAHOH 2 ^», WTlTfHAtcajNA 29 , Itfil iSSSK 


(2) They shall come into force on the date of their publication in the Official 
Gazette, 

2 . Number of posts, classlfloation and scale of pay.—The number of posts 
their classification and the scales of pay attached thereto, shall be as specified 
In columns 2 to 4 of the Schedule hereto annexed. 

3 . Method of recruitment, age limit and other quallflcatloMg.—The method of 
recruitment to the said posts, age limit, qualifications, and other matters con¬ 
nected therewith shall be as specified in columns 6 to 13 of the said Schedule; 

Provided that the upper age limit specified in column 6 Of the Schedule afore¬ 
said may be relaxed in the case of candidates belonging to Scheduled Castes 
Scheduled Tribes and other special categories of persons in accordance with the 
general orders issued from time to time by the Government of India, 

4 . Disqualifications.—No person,— 

(a) who has entered into or contracted a marriage with a person having 

a spouse living; or 

(b) who, having a spouse living, has entered into or contracted a marriage 

with any person, 

shall be eligible for appointment to any of the said posts: 

Provided that the Central Government may. If satisfied that such marriage 
Is permissible under the personal law applicable to such person and the Other 
party to the marriage and there are other grounds for so doing, exempt any 
person from the operation of this rule. 

6 , Power to relax.—Where the Central Government is pf opinion that it 1 « 
neces.sary or expedient to do so, it may, by order, for reasons to be recorded In 
writing, relax any of the provisions of these rules with respect to any class or 
category of persons.

MINISTRY OF PETROLEUM AND CHEMICALS AND MINES AND METALS 

(Department of Petroleum) 

New Delhi, the 20 ih February 1071 

S-O. 1103.— Whereas by a notification of the Government of India in the 
Ministry of Petroleum and Chemicals and Mines & Metals (Department of 
Petroleum) S.O. No. 3753 dated 26 th October, 1970 under sub-section ( 1 ) of 
Section 3 of the Petroleum Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in land) Act, 
1962 , the Central ciovernment declared its Intention to acquire the Right of User 
in the lands specified in the schedule appended to that notification for the purpose 
of laying pipelines; 

And whereas, the competent authority has, under sub-section ( 1 ) of section 0 
of the said Act. submitted report to the Government; 
appended to this notification; 

Now therefore, in exercise of the power conferred by sub-section ( 1 ) of the 
Section 6 of the said Act, the Central Government hereby declares that the right 
of user In the said lands specified in the schedule appended to this notification 
Is hereby acquired for laying for the pipelines; 

And further whereas, the Central Government has, after considering the said 
report; decided to acquire the Right of user in the land specified in the schedule 
shall instead of vesting in the Central Government vest on this date of the 
publication of this declaration in the Oil and Natural Gas Commission free from 
all encumbrances.

S.O. 1109 .—Whereas by a notification o,f the Government of India in the 
Ministry of Petroleum and Chemicals and Mines and Metals (Department of 
Petroleum) S.O. No. 3757 dated 27 th October. 1970 under sub section ( 1 ) of 
Section 3 of the Petroleum Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in land) Act, 
1902 (50 of 1962 ), the Central Government declared Its intention to acquire the 
Right of User In the lands specified In the schedule appended to that notification 
for the purpose of laying pipelines; 

And whereas, the competent authority has, under sub-section (1) of section 6 
of the said Act, submitted report to the Government; 

And further whereas, the Central Government has, after considering the said 
report, has decided to acquire the Right user in the lands specified in the schedule 
appended to this notification; 

Now therefore, in exercise of the power conferred by sub-section (1) of the 
Section 6 of the said Act, the Central Government hereby declares that the right 
of user in the said lands specified In the schedule appended to this notification 
is hereby acquired for laying the pipelines; 

And further, in exercise of the p.ower conferred by sub-section ( 4 ) of that 
section, the Central Government directs that the right of user in the said lands 
shall instead of vesting in the Central Government vest on this dote of the 
publication of this declaration In the Oil and Natural Gas Commission free from 
all encumbrances.

(Department of Miues and Metals) 

iNew Delhi, the 23 rd February 1971 

S.O. 1110 .—^Whereas by the notification of the Government of India in the 
Ministry of Petroleum and Chemicals and Mines and Me^ls (Department of 
Mines and Metals) S.O. No. 973 , dated the 3 rd March. 1970 ^ under sub-section 
( 1 ) of section 7 of the Coal Bearing Areas (Acquisition and Development) Act, 
1957 (20 Of 1957 ), the Central Government gave notice of its Intention to acquire 
the lands in the loca'llty specified in the Schedule appended to that notification. 

And whereas the competent authority in pursuance of section 8 of the said 
Act has made his report to the Central Government; 

And whereas the Central Government after considering the report of the 
competent authority and after consulting the Government of Madhya Pradesh, 
is satl.sfied that the land measuring 705.90 acres (approximately) or 286.62 hec¬ 
tares (approximately), out of the said lands, described in the Schedule append¬ 
ed hereto should be acquired; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section ( 1 ) of 
section 9 of the said Act, the Centi'al Government hereby declares that the lands 
measuring 705 00 acres (approximately) or 285.52 hectares (appioximately) des¬ 
cribed in the said schedule are hereby acquired. 

The plan of the area covered by this notification may be inspected in the 
Office of the Collector, Sidhl (M.P.), or in the Office of the Coal Controller, 1 , 
Council House Street, Calcutta or in the Office of the National Coal Develop¬ 
ment Corporation Limited (Revenue Section), Darbhanga House, Ranchi (Bihar).

New Delhi, the 18th February 1971 

S.O. 1153.—Tho following draft of a scheme further to amend the Cochin Dock 
Workers (Regulation of Itoployment) Scheme, 1959, which the Central Govern¬ 
ment proposes to make in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of 
section 4 of the Dock workers (Regulation of Employment) Act, 1948 (9 of 1948), 
is published as required by the said sub-section for the information of all persons 
likely to be affected thereby; and notice is hereby given that the .said draft will 
be taken Into consideration on or after the 13th March. 1971. 

Any objections or suggestions which may be received from any person with 
respect to the said draft before the date so specified will be taken Into considera¬ 
tion by the Central Government, 

Draft Scheme 

1. This Scheme may be called the Cochin Dock Workers (Regulation of 
Employment) Second Amendment Scheme. 1971. 

2. In clause 11 of the Cochin Dock Workers (Regulation of Employment) 
Scheme, 1059, in the proviso to item (g), for the words "upto rupees three 
hundred” the words “above rupees three hundred” shall be substituted. 

[No. 56/15/68-Fac.n.]

New Delhi, the 6th March 1971 

S.O. 1164.—In pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial Djsputes Act. 1947 
(14 of 1647), the Central Government hereby publishes the following award of the 
Arbitrator In the Industrial dispute between the employers In relation to the 
Beas Dam Project Administration, Talwara and its workman Shri Makhan Singh 
which was received by the Central Government on the ard March, 1971. 

BEFORE SHRI SHAM LAL, DY. LABOUR COMMISSIONER, PUNJAB 
(SINCE RETIRED), SOLE ARBITRATOR 

Parties ; 

Makhan Singh, ex-workman. 

Vs. 

Management of Bcas Dam Project 
PhesenI ; 

Shrl Sham Lai, Sole Arbitrator 


Appearances ; 

(1) Sbri A. R. Roy, Executive Engineer, Personnol—for the Manaaement 

(2) Shrl G. C. Bhalla, General Secretary, INTUC Punjab for Makhan Singh 

—concerned workman. 

AWARD 


An industrial dispute having arisen between Makhan Singh, a workman of the 
Management of Beas Dam Project Administration and the said Management 
consequent upon the dismissal of Makhan Singh from service, the oarlies ug-eed 
to arbitration of the dispute by the undersigned. The arbitration agreement 
drawn up under Section lOA of the l.D. Act, 1947 was published by the Secretary 
to Government Pb., Labour Department In the Punjab Governrnent Gazette 
(Extraordinary) vide Notification No. lOTB-SF-II-L&E-SL dated 
After holdinr' necessary proceedings and hearing the arguments put mrth by the 
parties the case was fully completed for decision when it came to the notice of 
the undersigned that the Punjab Government was not the ‘appropriate Gover^ent 
In relation to the said dispute under the l.D. Act and as such no jurisdiction 
was conferred on me to hold any proceedings and give my arbitration award in 
respect of the said dispute. Accordingly, the matter was referred by the Punjab 
Government authorities to the Government of India Ministry of Labour, E^loy- 
ment and Rehabilitation who publLshed the arbitration 

lOA of Industrial Disputes Act. 1947. between the parties the Gazette of India 
vide order dated 4th May, 1970, of which a copy was endorsed to me. ine arPitra- 
Uon Agreement having thus been properly published by the “aPProPrite Govern¬ 
ment”.** Ihe undersigned Issued usual notices to the parties to attend the arbitra 
tion proceedings on the prescribed place, date and time. In reply to the n 



Sec. 3(ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA; MARCH 20, 1971/PHALGlJNA 29. 1892 1377 


the pariiOs appeared and made statements before me that instead of recovdinjf 
the proccoaings denovo the arbitration award may be based on the ola proceed¬ 
ings, As sucii in view of the clear statements of the parties I (jeclded not to call 
tresh evidence and hear the parties de novo. The Issue referred for arbitration 
as mentioned in the arbitration agreement is reproduced below:— 

“Whether the dismissal of Shrl Makhan Singh is justified and m order? if 
net, to what relief/exact amount of compensation, he is ontitled?” 

Briefly stated the facts of the case are that Shri Makhan Singh was employed 
as a Chargeman in the Beas Dam Project Administration. It is alleged that on 
13ih September, 1966 at about 13.50 hours while on duty he was 'oimd sleeping 
toy Shri Kartar Singh, Executive Engineer, Concreting Division No. III. On eiiquu-y 
by Shri Kuitar Singh as to why he (Makhan Singh) was sleeping while on duty, 
Makhan Singh is stated to have denied the allegation and used improper language 
in an ins'.iltmg tone, whereupon he was placed under suspension and .subsequently 
charge sheeted. It has come on record that Makhan Singh refused to accept the 
charge sheet which was later on sent to him under Regd. A.D. cover and even 
then he did not accept it as per remarks of the postal authorities on the Registered 
A.D. envelope. The matter was referred by the Executive Engineer, Concreting 
Division No. Ill to an enquiry committee consisting of Shri G. C. Tanela, S.D.O. 
Material, Concreting Division No, I and Shri Balblr Singh, S.D.O. Field Concretlns 
Division No. 3. The Committee could not hold the enquiry as the second me'dloned 
officar proceeded on leave. Makhan Singh also objected to the enquiry being held 
by the said officers and on his request another Enquiry Committee consisting of 
Executive Engineer, Concreting Division No. 2 and Executive Engineer, Tunnelling 
Division No. 1 was constituted for the purpose. The Committee recorded the 
evidence adduced by the Deptt. and also by Makhan Singh and submitted its 
report to the Deptt. a copy of which has been brought on record D.y the respondent 
management as Exhibit M.W. 1/8. The Committee gave its findings as under:— 

1. Shri Makhan Singh mls-behaved to Shri Kartar Singh, Executive Engineer. 
Concreting Division No. 3 on 13th September, 1966 at about 13-50 hours and used 
improper language in an insulting tone. He did use the wording to the Executive 
Engineer, Concreting Division No. 3 “You are Grey haired old man. Why are 
you telling lies when I was not sleeping? You have children and I have children. 
Fear from God”. 

2, Shri Makhan Singh refused-to accept the charge sheet issued by the Execu¬ 
tive Engineer, Concreting Division No. 3, Talwara letter No. 4388/3-W, dated 13th 
September, 1966 when he was approached personally by the stall of the Sub 
Divisional Officer, Administration Concreting Division No, 3 on 14th September, 
1966 and 15th September, 1966 and later on also sent to him by post under 
registered cover. 

3, Shri Makhan Singh was not actually sleeping on I3th September, 1966, but. 
he was definitely idling away his time in the Pumping shed at Downstream face 
of T-2 Diversion Tunnel, 

4. Shri Makhan Singh was not assigned any definite job for the last many 
days and he was required only to note the names and tokens of tho outgoing 
workcharged stall so that they might not .spend more time outside the tunnel. 

.5. It was not a fit case of summary .suspension. Moreover, according to the 
certified standing orders applicable on this project an Executive Engineer is no^ 
competent to suspend a workman summarily Ignoring all the lormallties i.e, Issulm 
of chargesheet etc. 

6. Shri Makhan Singh In his statement has drawn the attention of the enquL 
committee regarding his presence i.e. ‘P’ reduced to P/4 on 3rd September, 190k 
by Shn Unrish Paul Sectional Officei: and in this connection he has produced » 
number of witnesses Jn his support that he was present from 1 P.M. to 6 P.M 
on 3rd September, 1966. According to Shri Makhan Singh, this point is the base 
for happening on 13th September, 1966. Apparently a workmen cannot be marked 
P or P/4 It he is absent in the first four hours of the shift. If he was not present 
during the first four hours he would have been marked absent for the whole shift. 
This point is beyond the scope of the enquiry committee and may kindly be dealt 
with at the required level. 

On the basis of the report of the Enquiry Committee the S.D.O. gave another 
charge sheet vide memo No. 454 dated 25th November, 1906, brought on re^rd 
as Exhibit M.W, 1/10, to Makhan Singh asking for reply upto 29th November. 
1966. A reply tu this memo was sent by Makhan Singh on 27th November, 1966. 



1378 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA. MARCH 20. )971/PHALGUNA 29, 1892 [Pahi II— 


denying the charges. MaKhan Singh was dismissed from service vide Memo. 
No. 1-5-b'E, dated aist January, 1967 addressed to Mahhan Singh, by XEN, Farm 
Works Division, Talwara. This gave rise to the Industrial dispute which has been 
submitted to me by the parties for arbitration. 

f htive recorded the evidence adduced by the parties and heard their argu- 
tneiUb. The Management have brought cn record, inter alia the charge sheet 
served on Makhan Smgh and alleged to have been refused by him even when 
sent to him under Regd. A.D. cover and the report made by the Enquiry Com¬ 
mittee. Shri Kaitar Singh, Executive Engineer, Concreting Division No. Ill and 
Makhan Singh workman concerned were also examined. Full opportunity was 
given by me to the parties to adduce evidence. A perusal oi the Enquiry Com¬ 
mittee’s report reveals that the following three witnesse.s listed by Shrl Makhan 
Singh could not be heard as they were absent from duty. 

1. Shri Nlkka Ram, Be’dar, Concreting Division No. 2. 

2. Shri Karam Singh SK/Lab Concreting Division No. 2. 

3. Shri Vlkiam Singh 8(h)-K Pumping Sub Division. 

Non-examination of the above witnes.ses is a serious irregularity constituting 
violation of the principles of natural justice. The purpose of the rules of natural 
justice IS to safeguard the position of the person against whom an enquiry is 
being conducted so that he is able to meet the charges laid against him properly. 
The workman concerned should be given a fair opportunity to examine his wit¬ 
nesses in defence. 

The reason given by the Enquiry Committee for not examining the tliree wit¬ 
nesses is hardly tenable. If the witnesse.s were not available on the particular 
date fixed for recording evidence in defence of the workman, the right thing for 
the Committee should have been to adjourn the proceedings to some other date 
insteaa of leaving them out. Nothing has come on record before me to show 
thrt any attempt was made by the Enquiry Committee to ensure attendance of 
the three wltnes.ses for recording their evidence Tne Enquiry Committee thus 
fai'ed to give Makhan Singh reasonable opportunity to lead evidence in his 
defence. The charge .sheet which was -served on Makhan Singh vide Memo. No. 1 
speclal/Adin. dated 13th September. 1969 reads as under;— 

“Please explain why disciplinary action should not be Uken against you 
as you were iound sleeping in one corner of Pumping Shed near hill 
side D/S T-2 on l.Jtb September, 1966 by Executive Engineer, Con¬ 
creting Divi,sion No. Ill at 13-,50 hours. On being asked for such gioss 
negligence of duty, you used abusive, filthy nnd improper language 
and said that Executive Engineer, No ill was telling lie and you 
were onl.y having re.st in the shade As such you have misbehaved 
the Executive Engineer, Concieting Dlvl.sion No. Ill wiUingly. 

Repl.y to thi.s charge sheet should reach this olTlce up to 17th September, 1966. 

(Sd.) KaRtar Singh 13-9-1966 
Exeputiyo Engineer, 
Concreting Division No. III.’’ 


The opening sentence of the above charge sheet undoubtedly shows that Shri 
Kartar Singh XEN, acted with » prejudiced mind and even before giving an 
opportunit.y lo Makhan Singh he had pre.iudiced the issue Besides, the charge 
of the use of abusive, filthy and Impropei language wa.s no! specified in the charge 
sheet. It was only alleged that Makhan Singh had replied that XEN concreting 
Division No. Ill was telling the lie and that he (Makhan Singh) wag having rest 
in the shade. 

The second cha-ge sheet served on Makhan Singh vide Memo. No. 454 dated 
25th November. 1536 by S.D.O. reads as under: — 

“please explain wny disciplinary action should noi be taken against you 
for the following olTence as came to light during the enquiry con¬ 
stituted in connection with the happening ot l-'lth September, 1,966 
by the Fnquiry C’ommlttee-— 

1. Refusal to accept charge sheet 

2. Idling away time 



Sec. THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: MARCH 20. 1971/PHALGUNA 29, 1392 1379 


3. Use of Improper and insulting language towards Executive Engineer 
as reported by the Enciulry Committee constituted under orders of 
S.E. Tunnelling vice his 3749-51/7-E, dated 28th September, 1960. 

Sd/- 

Sub-Divislonal Offleer, 
Bub Division 25-11-1960, 


It is not understood as to why it was decided to issue the .second charge sheet 
to Makhan Singh after the findings of the Enquiry Committee. The Department 
perhap.s intended the charge sheet to be a .show cause notice to the workman. 
Even If it were so, the Department ought to have clearly Indicated the punishment 
which it proposed to award to Makhan Singh. The order of dismissal from 
service conveyed to Makhan Singh vide Memo. No. l-!)- 6 E, dated 2nd January, 
1967 stales that he was “dismissed under rule 15(4) of Standing Orders cs certi¬ 
fied copy received under C.N.B.P. No. 11106-08/BP(A)3264/61, dated 251h April, 
1906 without notice or any compensation. In lieu oi notice” Curiously enough 
a copy Of the certified Standing Orders or of the relevant extract therefrom has 
not been brought In evidence. 


On examination of the evidence, I find that the Enquiry Committee violated 
the principles of natural justice by not giving full ooportunlty to‘ Makhan Singh 
inasmuch as three of his witnesses were not examined simply for the fact that 
they were absent. No reasons have been given in the report ns to why those wit- 
Ties.ses could not be examined on any subsequent date. Su.ely, the Committee 
could have postponed the enquiry proccedlng.s to any date when the witnesses were 
availahte. Secondly, the charge sheet was rather vague Inasmuch as It did not 
bring home to the worker concerned fully the charge against him. Thirdly no 
proper show cause notice was given to Makhan Singh. 

In view cJ these material inegularities committed in the holding of the enquiry, 
I hold that there has been violation of the princip’' 9 s of natural justice. That 
being the case, the order of dismissal of Makhan Singh is held to be unjustified 
and improner. I, therefore, hold that Makhan Singh Is entitled to leinstatement 
with continuity of service. 

Normally in ca.ses of reinstatement, the rule is to award full back wages for 
the period of forced unemployment of the workman lo the present case, no evi¬ 
dence ha.s been led by the parties to show whether Makhan Singh wa.s or was 
not employed during the period commencing from hi.s dismissal from .service. In 
these days of high prices, it is improbable that Mnkhan Plnph remained totally 
unemployed. He must have cr he ought to have strived to take out his existence, 
may he in very trying circumstances. Taking thi.s faclo- into consideration and 
.also considering the other factors including the loner nc’dod Involved. I feel that 
the ends of justice would he adequately met hy allowing Makhan Singh 7.5 per 
cent of hi.s wages for the period of forced unemployment. T, therefore, award 
reinstatement of Makhan Singh with 75 per cent of wage.s as compensation foi 
the period of imemnlnyment. 

The award is submitted to Government as required under Section 10A(4) t>f 
Industrial Disputes Act, 1947. 

(Sd.) Sham Lap, 
my. L.C. Pb. (Retired),

The said agreement was brought on record With the consent of the parties the 
hearing was adjourne'd for arguments on 11th February. 1971. I heard the 
arguments of both the parties on I2th February, 1971. 

3 The case of the workmen in brief as made out in their written statement i.s 
thart the management of Nag’s Ramjlwanpur Colliery had not Implemented the 
recommendations of the Coal Wage Board regarding payment of fall back wages 
to the underground loaders and whenever workmen agitated for securing proper 
implementation of the Coal Wage Board recommendations, the management came 
forward -with retrenchment notices and as a matter of fact the management had 
retrenched several workmen and subsequently recruited new persons; that there 
were discussien.s between the management and the union before the officers of 
the Central Industrial Relations Machinery on several occasions during which 
the Colliery Manager and other representatives of the management Ijad categorically 
assured that there would he no retrenchment of lowders: that all of a sudden the 
management served retrenchment notlce.s to 40 workmen retrenching them on and 
from 3rd August. 1970 in violation of their own written assurances; that the reasons 
for retrenchment as mentioned in the retrenchment notices were not true in- 
ff'^much as the production went on gradually Increasing since 1968 and at the same 
time the labour strength went on gradually decreasing; that the action of the

7. Now I examine the second point at issue, namely, whether the management 
of the colliery complied with all the relevant provisions of the Industrial Disputes 
Act before effecting the retrenchment; — 

B. Sectipn 25F of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 lays dciivn conditions 
precedent to retrenchment of workmen. Section 26F reads as under:— 

■‘25-F. Conditions precedent to retrenchment of workmen.—No workmen 

employed in any industry who has been In continuous service for not 
less than one year under an employer shall he retrenched by that 
employer until— 

IB} the Workman has been given one month’s notice in writing indicating 
the reasons for retrenchment and theCpcriod of notice has expirea, 
or the workman has been paid In lieu of such notice wages for the 
period of the notice. 

Provided that no such notice shall be necessary if the retrenchment is under 
an agreement which specifies a date for the termination of servdee; 

to) the workman has been paid, at the time of retrenchment, compensation 
which sliall be equivalent to fifteen days’ average pay for every 
completed year of continuous service or any part thereot in excets 
of six months; and 

(c) * ♦ * e 

It is a settled point in law that the provisions contained in clauses (a) and (b) 
of Section 26F of the Acf are mandatory provisions and tliese provisions have 
to be satft'ffS'd before a workman can be retrenched. I am reproducing the decision 
of the Supreme Court in the case of Bombay Union of Journalists Vs. State of 
Bombay. (1964) I LLJ 351 at 357:— 

"Similarly, Clause (b) piovldes that the workmen has to be paid, at the time 
of retrenchment, compensation which shall be equivalent to fifteen uays 
average pay tor every completed year of service, or any part thereof 
in excess of six months. It would be noticed that this payment has 
to be made at the time of retrenchment, and this requirement again 
provides a safeguard, in the interests of the workman; he must be 
given one month’s notice or wages in lieu thereof and he must get 
retrenchment compensation as prescribed by clause (b). The object 
which the legislature had in mind In making these two conditions obli¬ 
gatory and in constituting them Into conditions precedent is obvious. 
These provisions have to be satisfied before a workman can be 
letrencned.” 

While the learned representative of the management accepted this position, he 
argued that the relevant provisions contained in clauses (a) and (b) of Section 25F 
of the Act have been fuLy complied with In as much as .the management offered 
to pay one month’s pay in lieu of notice and retrenchment compensation due to the 
workmen concerned under letters dated 29th July 1970 from the Manager of the 
colliery addressed to each of the loaders concerned which were sent to them under 
registered post with acknowledgment due vide Ext. M. 25 to M. 64. It is an 
admitted case of the parties that aJ the loaders in question had put In not less than 
one year’s continuous service and that the retrenchment was not under any agree¬ 
ment- The management ha.s neither adduced an.y oral evidence nor any documentary 
evidence to substantiate that the manager’s letters dated 29th July 1970 were 
despatched under re.glstered post with acknowledgement due to the workmen con¬ 
cerned, There is nothing to show as to on which date or dates those letters were 
actually despatched. It is curious to note that whereas the management has pro¬ 
duced as many as 70 documents, they have not thought is prudent to produce the 
postal registration receipts to Indicate thq date or dates of registration as well as the 
postal acknowledgement receipts to Indicate the dates on which the manager’.s 



1388 the gazette of INDIA; MARCH 20. 197I/PHALGUNA 29. 1892 [Pari 


letters cited above were received by the workmen concerned. There is no evidence 
to show that one month s notice pay and the retrenchment compen-satipn were 
actually kept ready tor disbursement tg the workmen concerned in the office ol the 
colliery on 3rd August tUVO as indicated m the manager’s setters dated 2iith July 
1970. Even the documents showing the amount ot one months wages in lieu ot 
hotice and the amount of retrenchment compensation payable to workmen concerned 
in accordance with clause (b) ot Section 26P of the Act purported to have been 
ottered to the wortcmen has not been produced betorp me for reasons best known 
to the management. Even in respect of '1 loaders In question to whom according 
to the management one month s nonce pay and retrenchment compensation had been 
paid, no document has been produced to substantiate their contention. Neither 
of the management’s witness has stated the amount actually paid to those lOaders 
and the dates of payment. It Is, however, not the case of the management that 
when the remaining loaders did not come to receive the payments referred to above, 
the money was remitted to them. In my opinion, mere sending of notices calling 
upon the workmen to receive the payment on or before the due date will not suffice 
and by doing so the employers cannot ciaim to have complied with the mandatory 
provisions gf clauses fa) and fb) of Section 25F of the Act. In this connection I invite 
attention to the decision of His Lordship P. C. Pandit in Pepsu Transpoit Co. (P) 
Ltd. Vs. State of Punjab (1968 A.I.R. P. 90 Punj.). The relevant extract of his 
decision is reproduced below; — 

"A plain reading of the provisions of this section shows that there are two 
conditions which have to be satisfied by the employer before he can 
retrench a workman who had been In continuous service for not less 
than one year in his industry. The first is that he should he given one 
month’s notice in writing mentioning the reasons for his retrenchment 
and the period of notice had expired or If no such notice was given, 
then he should be paid in lieu thereof wages for the period of notice. 
The second is that the workman should, at the time of retrenchment, 
be paid compensation which would be calculated ip accordance with 
the principle laid down In sub-section (b) above, It is the obligation 
Of the employer tg fulfil both these conditions before he can validly 
retrench the workmen. The time of retrenchment would, in the case 
of workman, who had been given one month’s notice, be at the end 
of that period and in the case of the workman, who was to be paid 
wages for the period of the notice in lieu of such notice, It would be 
the one fixed by the employer. 

If the said workman docs not come to receive it on or before the due date, 
when called upon to do so. the employer should send the same to him 
on that date, if possible otherwise on the next day and it is only then 
that it can be said that he complied with the condition laid down in the 
section. 

Mere sending notice.s calling upon the workmen to receive payment before the 
due date and then equating such an oiler to actual payment might lead 
to harsh results because if the employee could not come on the day 
fixed to receive the payment for seme good reason, an obstinate em¬ 
ployer may refuse to make the payment on the next day on the plea 
that the notice Itself was equivalent to payment and his obligation to 
make the payment had ceased on the previous day.” 

As already stated earher, there is nothing on record to show that the management 
had compiled with the mandatory provisions of the clauses (a) and (b) of Section 
25F of the Industrial Di,sputes Act, 1947 which lays down condltiohs precedent to 
retrenchment of the workmen. This failure of the management to comply with the 
mandatory provisions of the lajv gives fatal blow to their case, I, therefore, hold 
that the retrenchment of 39 loaders in question was bad on the ground that the 
provisions of clauses (a) and (b) of Section 25F of the Act were not complied with 
by the management before e/Tectlng the retrenchment. 

9. Section 25G of the Industriai Disputes Act, 1947 jays down procedure for 
retrenchment, which reads as under; — 

“25-G. Procedure for retrenchment -—^Where any workman in an Industrial 
establishment, who is a citizen of India, is to be retrenched and he 
belongs to a particular category of workmen In that establishment. In 
the absence of any agreement between the employer and the workman 
in this behalf, the employer shall ordinarily retrench the workman who 
was the last person to be employed in that category, unless for reasons 
to be recorded the employer retrenches any other workman.” 

It Is nobody’s case that the loaders in question were not the citizens of India and 
that there was any agreement between the management and the loaders concerned

Bainjibanpur Col'iery P.O. Sitarampur, Dist. Burdwan in retrenching the 39 loaders 
named in the arbitration agreement referred to above with effect from 3rd Augus 
1970 was not justified. A.s a matter of fact in the eyc.s of law there was no retrench¬ 
ment of those 39 londer.s. I direct that the management of Nag’.s iRamiibanpur 
Colliery shall allow the 39 loader.s in question to resume their duties as ear’y a= 
possible but in any case not later than ten days from the date on which the award 
become enforceable and pay them full wages, bonus, allowances etc., withip fifteen 
from the date on which the award become enforceable and also give them 
all benefits treating them to be on duty during the period of their forced unemploy¬ 
ment. 


11- The arbitration award is made accordingly and submitted to the Central 
Government under Section 10A(4) of the Industrial Dispute.s Act, 1947. 

(Sd-) K- Sharan.

4. Having thus cleared the ground of numerical uncertainties, I now turn to 
examine the contents of the two written statements filed by the parties. On behalf 
of the workmen, their trade Union, the Colliery Mazdoor Sabha. filed a written 
statement. In paragraph 3 of the said written statement, it was pleaded that 
all the workmen named In the order of reference were employed In or about 
1962-63 and after continuous service for several years, without any adverse re¬ 
mark, they were all given permanent statu-s, in accordance with the Standing 
Orders of the Company. It appears from paragraphs 4, 5 and 6 of the said 
written statement that there were intertrade-unlon rivalries between the then 
existing trade unions in the colliery, dominated by different political parties, 
which created an unfortunately dangerous situation. Disgusted wllli the existing 
state of affairs, the workmen intended to join another trade union of the name 
of Colliery Mazdoor Sabha, led also by another political party called Communist 
Party of India (Marxist), Paragraphs 9, 10 and 11 of the said written statement 
describe how the management and the two former trade unions of the colliery 
joined hands together in order to prevent the workmen from joining a trade union 
dominated by Communist Part.y of India (Marxists) and how they succeeded in 
letting loose a reign of terror. In the months of April—May, 1969. The language used 
in paragraphs 10 and 11 may be .somewhat flamboyant, but I better set out the para¬ 
graphs herein below; 

"10. Ab a matter of fact goondas were hired financed by the management 
of the said colliery to terrorise the aforesaid workmen and in fact in

the month of April and May a reign of terror was created. Workmen 
concerned were forced to leave the Dhowra where they used to 
reside, jJnowras were set on fiie, slabbllng, bombing, arson, 'ootjrg, 
we.e the da.y to day .--tory of the said colliery both Inside and outside. 

11. The terror and torture created and done with the Initiative ol the 
rnanngemcnt of the said colliery Is not only brutal but unprecedented 
In the history of civilised tree nations.’’ 

The result was, it was alleged, that the workmen had to flee irom their dhowras 
for Saving their lives and camps had to be opened to give them shelter. Para¬ 
graphs Hi and 14 give the narpes of per.sons who were either beaten up or killed, 
and in paragraph 1.1 It is described how the management made it absolutely 
impos.slble for the workmen to report for duty, ‘‘despite their earnest and sincere 
efforts to do so”. In paragraph IG It was pleaded;

7 Ml. Malkliandy, learned Advocate lor the workmen, submitted In the firsw

there was sufficient justification for their absence. I have aiready quoted the le- 
levant extracts from the written statement flied by the parties. That there exisled 
inter-trade union rivalries in the colliery is not a rnatlcr oi much dispute. What l.s 
disputed by the management is that the allegation by the workmen that the 
management at all encourage such rivalries, in o,der to preve A the workmen 
from joining Qolllery Mazdoor Sabha, a trade union alleged to ue dominated by 
the Communist Party ol India, There is not much evidence befOie me supporiiiig 
the involvement of the management in the persLstent inter-union rjvalrie.s and the 
consequent hooiiganlsm that admittedly followed It l not necersary for me to 
find out, in the pre.sent context, how far the management was exploiting the rival- 
ties between the diffeient trade unions of the workmen lor their own pui poses. The 
ftinry in the writleii statement ol the workmen that the management hired goondas 
to terrorise the workmen was not established by evidence. I have already describ¬ 
ed the language us flamboyant. Be that as it may. suffice it for m.y purpose that 
there existed m the colliery, sufficient bad blood between different trade unions 
of the workmen, cau.sing clashes amongst them and resulting in .serious menace to 
human live.s The woikmen pleaded, in their written statenieni, thst in the months 
of April and May, 1969 “a reign of terror was created’. The inanag''ment also 
p’earied, in their written statement, that from the beginning of the year 1969 , a 
ten.sion grew up amongst the different' section of workmen employed and residing 
in the colliery and on April 11 , 1969 ‘‘a big violent clash took place m the colliery 
between two groups of workmen resulting to severe injuries to many workmen who 
took part in the said clash”. The management also admitted In Ihc written state- 
menl that thp tnesion caused by the fighting, “demoralised all the workmen resid¬ 
ing m the colliery and many workmen left the colliery on the date qf occurrence 
and on subsequent dates thereafter cither with leave or without k'ave. I there¬ 
fore agree with Mr. Malkhandy to this extent that in April-May, 1969 the .situation 
in the colliery was very unsafe and demoralised workmen began to run away from 
the colliery nut of fright. 

R The management, however, wanted to show that “normalcy" returned .some¬ 
time thereafter and those who bad fled in terror mostly returned, excepting the 
concerned workmen. Now, this return of normal condition in the colliery is not 
admitted by the workmen. According to them, the Invitation of the management 
asking the workmen to reporl for duty was made at a time when situation was 
still unsafe for the workmen to return lo work and therefore they could not return. 
The management exploited this situation, Issued chargesheets again.st the workmen, 
observed empty formalities of enquiry and discharged the workmen, whom they 
always wanted to throw away on account of their inclination to join the trade 
union known as Colliery Mazdoor Sabha.

12. Now. having considered the evidence on recoid. I find that there prevaTed a 
good dpal of insecurity in the coilierv in the months of April-May, 1969 That 
caused many workmen to run away from their place of work tor sffoty. Whether 
the Insecurity was of such magnitude as justified the workmen to run away to their 
native homes is difficult to decide on the evidence before me. But I am pieiaicd 
to ho d that the hooliganism was sufficient to make the workmen panicky and that 
caused the mass exodus. Therefore, there may have been some nushficalion for 
their running away In April-May 1969. But the further question arises whether 
having run away to the security of other places, they should have hybernated there, 
without caring to come back or without keeping Information as te whether their 
place of work was safe enough for return to duty. The turther story by one of 
the witnesses of the workmen that the management prevented willing workmen 
ffom rejoining their dulies through their own Chaprasis and durwans, I am not 
prepared to believe. This story, in that form, was not even pleaded by the work¬ 
men in their written statement. That is the version of only one workman and in 
my opinion an exaggerated version. It appears from Ex. P. (a letter wriltcn by

letter written by workman named Awadhar Rajbhur to his trade union on May 19

by them was that they were not getting back their work. It further appears from 
Fx I (a letter written by .some workmen to the Manager of the Colliery on May

ment I, therefore, hold that at the time when the chargesbeets for absenteei.sm 
began to be issued to ihc workmen, there was no m.stiflcation for absence from 
long prior to the date of chargesheets and the workmen were absenting themselves 
at their own peril, 

19 Before I leave this point, I peed refer to certain case laws which Mr. 
Majkhandy labourouslv found out and recited In extenso. The first cose leTed I'pon 
hi, him is a decision o( the Court of Appeal in Woodlev vs. The MetTOpoliian Dis- 
tric Railway Company, (1877) 2 Ex. Div. 384. In that case, the plaintifC, a workman 
in the employ of a contractor engaged by the defendant, had to work in a dark 
tunnel rendered dangerous by the passing of trains. After he had been working a 
Tortnlght he was In.iured by a passing train. The jury found that the defendant 
wa.c not adopting any precautions for the protection of the plaintiff and had been 
guilty of negligence. Clockbiirn. C. J. Mellor mid Grove, JJ agreeing with him 
held by maiorlty, reversing the decision of the Court of Exchequer, that the plaintiff 
having continued in his emploinnent with full knowledge, could not make the defen¬ 
dants liable for an injury arising from dancer to which he had voluntarily exposed 
himself. In that background. Mellor J, expressed the following opinion, upon which 
Mr. Malkhandy strongly relied- 

"Now, whether the master has done anything which may make him liable 
as between himself and the plaintiff we are not concerned to decide. 
Priestly V Fowler 8 M. & W 1. which is a leading case on the subject,

the pilnclple upon which this case may be decided. In that case it 
was said by Lord Abinger In delivering the judgment of the Court, 
“The mere relation of master and servant can never Implv an obliga¬ 
tion on the part of the master to take more care of the servant thin

he may reasonably be expected to do of himself. He Is no doubt pf bound 
to provide for the safety of the servant in the course of his employment 
to the best of his judgment, information, and belief. The servant is 
not bound to risk his safety in the service of his rpaster, and may, 
if he thinks fit, dec ine any service in which he reasonably apprehends 
danger to himself, and in moat cases In which danger ma,v be Incurred,

In the present case the plaintiff had probably the same opportunity of judging

Taken or means provided by his master, but, as it appears to nie, that

I do not see lor what purpose Mr. Malkhandy recited the above passage. This is 
not a .suit for damages for negligence. The workman is free cither to work or not 
to work. The question for my consideration is whether the w'orkmen were justilled 
ill absenting themselves at will and then claiming reinstatement on dismissal. I 
have, in the facts of this case, held that the absence itsef from long prior to the 
date of the chargesheet was unjustified. Therefore, the recitation made by Mr. Mal¬ 
khandy is not at all appropriate.

posed to be meted out (o him ” 

In my opinion, a great majority of workmen were not given the reasonable oppor¬

(a) Publication of the notice of enquiry in a newspaper ls not the most desirab'e

for a notice to workmen. 

(c) Assuming for the sake of argument that where all methods of reaching the 
workmen failed, thi.s form of service of notice may be treated a.s the best form. In

in some newspaper in the regional language with wide circulation (1961) I ITJ 303,

19. In order to get rid of this difficulty, Mr. K. P. Mukherjee had two further

I am not, sure whether. In the instant case, where there was not such bar, the work¬ 
men might not have attended if they had been given sufficient notice of the charges 
againEt them and the dates of enquiry. 

20 Mr. Mukherjee contended in the next place that the management had waited 
for a long time for the workmen to return and If they had not returned, that was 
at their own peril. The management could not stop mining work indefinitely for 
them and go on holding a protracted enquiiy for the purpose of dismissing the work¬ 
men tor h ameworthlness flr.st of all and then starting their business. I find some 
force in the argument that where workmen en masse absent themselves from work 
without cause, tho management is at liberty to carry their own work by any fair 
means In their control. Here, however, the case of the management is that except¬ 
ing the several workmen named In the order of Reference, others had returned and 
were working. The work of the management was not stopped, Therefore, the second 
tireach ol argument of Mr. Mukherjee is an exaggerated argument and I do not 
make much ot it.

The C.M.S. (AITUC), Ranlganj raised an I.D- in their letter dated 2titn July, 
1969 over the alleged stoppage of work of 92 workmen by the manage¬ 
ment of Patmohana Colliery who had reported for duty on 7th July, 
1)69. Notice for conciliation proceedings was given to the parties on 
26th Juiy, 1960 itself for holding conciliation proceedings on 11th August. 
1969. However, the parties approached the A.L.C (C). Asansol for 
inflating the conciliation proceedings earlier and as a lesult after due 
Intimation to them the conciliation proceedings were he’d on 5th 
August, 1969. During the course of these proceedings the parties agreed 
to settle the dispute on the following terms; 

1. It is agreed thai al’ these workmen shall report for duty within five (5) 
days from date to the Colliery Manager and the management shall 
provide them the Jobs on which they were employed. 

V The services of these workmen shall be treated as continuous and the 
ouestlon regarding the treatment of the period of forced idleness shall 
be discussed mutually later by the parties- 

3. The management .shall provide them quarters in which they were resid¬ 

ing previously.” 

This compromis 3 was not disputed In the written statement but in course of 
giving evidence J R. Dhoop, the then Manager, one of the signatories of the agree¬ 
ment. a.leged that he had been physically abducted to the office of the Assistant

Labour Commissioner and forced to put his signature on this agreement. He went 
further and staled that he had divulged his plight before the Assistant Labour 
Commissioner, but nevertheless the agreement came to be. This made me cuiious, 
because I could not conceive that the Assistant Labour Commissioner would put 
his irnpramateur on Ex 30(n), knowing full we 1 thSt it was being forclbiy extracted 
out of Ihe manager. I. therefore, summoned the Assistant Labour Commissioner 
ns Court witness. He, however, denied the story that J. ft. Dhoop had complained 
of his plight or anything to him. I therefore do not make much of the evidence 
of J. E. Dhoop and the story of Ex. 3t)(a) having been forcibly extracted cut of him- 
Mr- K. P. Mukherjee. however, made a short submission op Ex, 30(aj. He did not 
dispute the formation of Ex. 30(a) as a valid agreement. He however submitted 
that the workmen did not honour their part of the agreement as In the exhibit and 
therel.'re Ihe rrianngoment wb.s not in obligation to honour their part of the agree¬ 
ment. In this submission Mr. Mukherjee is right. I hold, therefore, that £x. ;-iO(a) 
hoas not In any way help the workmen. 

22. In the re.su t, I hold that although the management might have been other¬ 
wise justified in disrnlssing the workmen for long absence from duty without ,1ust 
cause at the time when chargesheets were issued, the order of dismissal upon them 
was pot justified because the enquiry into their misconduct had not been pi oierly 
conducted and the entire proceedings was vitiated by violation of rules of natural 
ju.sti -t' in ro far aj the workmen, whose names appear in the Schedule to this 
award, were not given sufficient notice of the enquiry. 

S.T My award, therefore, is that the management of Patmohana Colliery of 
Messr.s Patmohana and Bharatchak Collieries Private Limited, were not Justified in 
dismissing/discharging from services t04 workmen mentioned In the Schedule here¬ 
to annexed from the respective dates shown against each of them in the order of 
reference, They are entitled lo reinstatemeni. I do not however make any award of 
back wages to them becau.se there is nothing to show that they were whoily un¬ 
employed during the entire period So far as the workmen, whose name.s dn not 
appear in the Schedule heieto annexed, are concerned, their dismissals were justified 
and they are not entitled to any relief. 

This Is my award.

New Delhi, the 11th March 1971 

S.O. 1169.—In pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 
(14 of 1947), the Central Government hereby publishes the following award of 
the Central Government Industrial Tribunal, Hyderabad, in the industrial dispute 
between the employers in relation to the Singarenl Collieries Company Limited, 
Post Office Kothagudium (Andhra Pradesh) and their workmen, wnlch was re¬ 
ceived by the Central Government on the 4th March, 1971. 

BEFORE THE INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL (CENTRAL) AT HYDERABAD. 
Phksent: 

Sri T. Chandrasekhara Reddy, BA., B.L., Chairman, Irdu.'trial Tribunal, 
Andhra Pradesh, Hyderabad. 

iNDusTgiAL Disputes No. 3 or 1970 


Between; 

Workmen of Singareni CoUteries Company Limited, Bellampalh 

And 

Management of Singareni Collieries Company Limited, Bellampalll. 
Appearances; 

Sri M. Komarlah, General Secretary, Slnganreni Collieries Workers’ Union, 
Kothagudem for Workmen. 

Sri K. Srinivasa Murthy, Secretary. Federation of Chamber of Commerce 
and Industry and Sri M, Shyam Mohan, Personnel Officer, Singareni 
Collieries Company Limited, Bellampally, for Management. 

AWARD 

The Government of India by Its Order dated 13th January 1970 Issued through 
the Ministry of Labour, Employment and Rehabilitation (Department of Labour 
and Employment) referred the dispute between the employers in relation to 
Singareni Collieries Company Limited. Kothagudem and their workmen to my 
predecessor (Sri Mohammed Najmuddln) for adjudication. Subsequently after 
his retirement the above dispute was referred to me for adjudication- The dispute 
referred for adjudication Is this:— 

Whether the management of Singareni Collieries Company Limited, Belam- 
palli is justified in denying the wages of new Category V to Shri K- 
Posham, Shift Electrician, Morgans Pit? If not, to what relief the 
workmen is entitled? 














Sec. 3 Cii)J IHE GAZETTE OF INDIA: MARCH 20 , 1971 /PHALGL)NA 29 . 1892 1409 


2 . The Workman filed a claims statement throueh Sri B, Gangaram, the Vico 
President ol tfte aingarem CoMeries Woriters’ Union, Belampain, aliesinjj that 
iC. Posham has been working as a shiit Electrician since a long time, l.e., even 
oetore loth August, 1987 , that apart irom 3 electricians 01 tne general shin, there 
were six electricians in the Morgans Pit In which there were two sections and in 
eacn section there were tnree shltt electricians in three shilts and that out of 
six electricians, nte shift electricians were paid wages in the new category V 
while K. Posham was given only the wages ol new Category IV although the 
nature of work turned outTijr him was similar or same as that of the other filve 
shut electricians, ine circular dated 4 th Decemoer, 1987 issued oy the Manager, 
Morgans Pit, to all the shift electricians including K. Posham, wherein his duties 
and responsibilities had been described also shows that Posham was a shift 
electrician. According to them, when representations were made through letter 
dated 7 th February, 1968 to the Management In that connection, K. Posham was 
removed from the shills and placed in the general shift under the Assistant 
Engineer with effect from 23 rd May, 1968 . So they averred that it was an act 
of victimisation dfshe with the object of denying the workman the wages of new 
Category V. However, they contended that even thereafter he was working 
independently and doing similar jobs as before and was given the assistance of 
a helper and a mazdoor, Therefare it was Incorrect to say, as contended by the 
Management, that K. Posham was attending to minor repairs to the machines- 
So they prayed that TC. Posham should be given wages of new Category V with 
retrospective effect’from 15 th August, 1967 , the date when the "Wage Board Re¬ 
commendations were implemented. 

3 . The Management filed a counter statement alleging infer alia that K. Posham 
was working as electrician in Category IV in Morgans Pit, that as per the Labour 
Appellate Tribunal Award electricians were placed in Categories IX to TV, that 
he was working as an electrician in Category IV old as per the Award of t'ne 
Labour Ajipeliate Tribunal previously, that after the implementation ol the Wage 
Board Recommendations the above mentioned six groups of electricians were 
placed in three groups, viz., electricians in old Categories IX and VIII were given 
new Category VI, electficians of old Categories VII and VI were given new 
Category V and electricians of old Categories V and VI were given new Category IV. 
According to the Management, It was the practice to have atleast one Category V 
(new) electrician in each shift and one or more Category IV (new) electricians 
In each shift according to the requirements of work- As Category V electrician 
was senior to Category IV electrician. Category V electrician was put incharge of 
the shift and the other electricians worked under him but there w-as no designation 
of shift electrician either In the L.A.T. Award or In the Wage Board Recommenda¬ 
tions with the result that all the electricians working in shills would be called 
shift electricians, They denied that K. Posham ever worked as Shift Incharge 
electrician ard that he was transferred on the representations of the Vice President, 
Slngarenl Collieries Workers Union, Bellampalll. They asserted that the circumst¬ 
ance that he was transferred on 23 rd May, 1968 and not immediately after 7 th 
February, 1968 the date of representation maide by the Vice President of the 
Union, proved that the transfer was made on accourt of necessity and not with 
any ulterior motive or os an act of victimisation. 

4 . The Management averred that trt the time when the dispute was raised 
there were six electricians In Category V, one In Category VI and four in 
Category TV on the rolls of the Morgans Pit, whereas now there ace six electricians 
In Category V, one in Category IV and one In Category VI, that the number of 
Category V electricians was in excess of the requirement because all electricians 
Ui Categories V and VI (old) were given new Category V with the result that 
there were more than one Category V electrician working in the same shift. So 
the senior-most Category V electricians were Incharge of the shifts, while others 
lij the same category worked under Kim and were counted against the sanctioned 
strength of Category IV electrician- They alto pleaded that while the sanctioned 
strength of Category V electricians In the Morgans Pit was six, the number of 
V Category electricians at that time was also six. So there was no vacancy in 
Category 'V Electricians In the Morgans Pit and the Management could not be 
compelled to increase the cadre strength to create a post for K. Posham in the 
higher category as fixation of cadre strength in the number of posts was entirely 
the management’s function. Further, The area of operation of the Morgans Pit 
had come down and if had become a small mine. Therefore even the present 
strength of electricians in that pit was not required. 

5 In regard to the Circular dated 4 th December. 1967 referred to in paragraph 4 
of the claims statement and Issued by the Management, It was alleged that It was 
addressed to all the Electricians working in shifts and it contained instructions 



1410 IHE gazette of INDIA: MARCH 20 , 1971 /PHALGUNA 29 , 1892 [Part II— 


legarding the work to be attended to by Electricians of Category V and IV. The 
Management further averred that the Wage Board Recommendations cannot be 
enforced and were Ulegal and void with the result that no claim can be based 
on them 

6. The only Issue for determination is whether the Management of the Slngareni 
Collieries Company Limited, BelarnpalJI, is Justifled in denying the wages of new 
Category V to Sri K. Posham, shift electrician, Morgans Pit? 

7 As had been mentioned above, while the Union contends that K, Posham 
was doing the work of new category electrician, the Management denied that he 
was doing the work of new Category V Electrician. The Company also denied 
that K. Posham was a shift electrician. In order to appreciate the contentions 
of the parties, it is necessary to deal with the evidence let in by both parties 
On behalf of the workers three Witnesses have been examined, while two witnesses 
have been examined on behalf of the Management. 

8. W.W. 1 is K. Posham in respect of the wages of whom this dispute has been 
raised. His evidence is that he had put in a service of itf years in the Company, 
that he worked as a mazdoor in the beginning for 2 years and thereafter as a 
helper for six years and 'was working as electrician later- According to him, he 
was In IV Category in February, 1084 apd was even now in Category IV- He 
was working In January, l' 9 S 5 as a shift electrician and he knew the work of 
Category V electrician and was working independently. He says he was given 
the assistance of a helper. The Ikiginder and the Supervisor aUotted work to him 
and according to him six persons were working as shift electricians. Prior to the 
Implementation of the Wage Board Recommendations of them he was the only 
electrician who was in Category IV. He would say that himself, J. Perumal and 
K. Sriharl were working in each shift by rotation. He worked as Vth Category 
electrician for 3 months in alternative weeks and even later he worked as Vth 
Category for six months contlnously. But he admitted that whenever he worked 
as V Category electrician he was given acting allowance. He fll^d Ex. W 1 the 
notice dated 4th December 1967 issued by the Manager to all the shlit electricians 
Including himself. But 'while the other two electricians working in the other 
shifts were in the old VII Category since 1965 and were placed in the r;ew 
Category V after the implementation of the Wage Board Recommendations, he 
alone was placed In IVth Category. He also filed Px. W 2 the programme for 
changing drilling machines for weekly repairs Issued to him describing him as 
Electrician Category VII by the Assistant Engineer. Even prior to the implemen¬ 
tation of the Wage Board Recommendaitlons he made a representation to the 
superiors and he was toTH that he would be placed m the Vth Category after the 
Implementation of the Wage Board Recommendations but that was not given 
Insplte of the representation. So he moved the Union which took up his cause 
but the Msnaigemept transferred him to the general shift and he was working in 
the general shift from the year 1988 . How'ever, he would say that even there 
he was working independently and was also given the assistance of a helper. But 
he admitted that he did not have any order appointing him as a .shift electrician 
and that he was in Category IV as per the Majumdar Award and that after the 
Implementation of the Wage Board Recommendations, the employees who were 
in Category V were placed in Category IV ngw. He also admitted that he had 
not been placed in Category Vll (old) even prior to the implementation of the 
Wage Board Recommendations although Ex. W 2 described him as an electrician 
of Category VII Hence the description in Ex. V/ 2 as an electrician In Category VII 
would not be of any avail to him. On his own showing he had no electrician’s 
certificate and cannot repair a motor completely. He denied a suggestion that he 
had not worked as V Category electrician previously and that Dusa> Rayallngu 
was supervising his work. 

9 . W.W. 2 , Perumallu, is a shift electrician. He was working as such since 
November, 1965 in the Morgans Pit, According to him electricians who rotate in 
shifts are cal’ed shift electricians. Hig evidence is tliprt Srihari and W.W. 1 
K. Posham, we’e the other shift electriclams working in that pit, that W.W. 1 was 
working ns .shift ejectriclan from the time, he loined uptll 1963 contlnuouslv and 
that Srihari and himself were in Category V while W.W. 1 was in IVth Category 
(new) although W.W. 1 wais doing the same work as hlm'.elf and Srihari. So 
W.W. 1 represented to the Engineer that he should be given Vth Category and the 
witness and others supported W.W. 1 . Then W.W, 1 was given acting allowance- 
He denied that W.W. 1 worked under a Category V electrician- Although he stated 
that a helper was sent to help the Vth Category electriclaai and In case a helper 
wa.s not available a mazdoor was sent to assist him, he admitted that the same 
facility was extended even to a TVth Category electrician. He did not know If 
promotion to a higher category was only when a vacancy arose and v/hether there 






Stc. 3 {ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: MARCH 20 , 1971 /PHALGUNA 29 . 1892 1411 


were vacancies of Vth Category electricians in th? Morgans Pit. He stated that 
some workers were transferred to Somagu.dem mines and promoted but did not 
know as to wny they were transferred. 

10 . W.W. 3 is Gangaram the Vice President of the Singarenl Collieries Workers' 
Union. He deposed that he attended the conciliation meeting held In connection 
with the case of WTW. 1 - He filed Ex. W 3 a copy of the proceedings of tne 
conciliation received by the Union and Eje, W 4 letter dated 27 th March, men 
addressed to the Union by the Agent, Belampalll Division and Ex. W 5 a letter 
addre.ssed by their Union to tlfe'I^ional Commissioner (Central). According to 
him, W.W. f was transferred from Shift Incharge on 23 rd May, 1968 to the general 
shut but he asserted that even thereafter W W. 1 was doing the same work 
which he was doing previously. According to the witness promotions were made 
taking into consideration the seniority and experience and this was observed in 
June, 1970 - Even according to him, their Union was not a party to the Trades¬ 
men’s agreement and it had been entered Into between ihe Management and the 
Tandur Collieries Workers Union In 1961 . In June, 1970 the management issued 
a notice under Section 9 A of the I. D. Act but their Union was contesting it. He 
admitted that he did not know that in the first shift, i.e-, general shift, the 
Chaigehand and Oth Category Electrician worked, and that some electiiclans of 
Morgans Pit were transferred to Somagudem Mines. 


3 . The Management filed a counter statement alleging inter alia that K. Posham 
to that he w»s working at Kothagudem In the Singortnl Collieries Company. He 
knew W W. T. His eiddence Is that electrician in Morgans Pit were in four 
categories prior to the Wage Board Recommendations, one In Category IX, one 
In Category VII, one in Category TV and they were given new categories after the 
Implementation of the Wage Board Recommendations. The old IX Category 
Electrician was placed in Category VI new and the old Category VH electrician, was 
placed in Category V new and old Category IV electrician is placed in Category IV 
new. According to him, there was no post designated as shift electrician and all 
electriclan.s who wo rking in shifts by turns are called shift electricians and 
M.W. 1 says all electftWans including Category IV electricians work in shifts 2 
and 3 report about their work to the chargehamd. If the IVth Category electricians 
are In the general shift, they report about their work to the Vlth Category 
electrician and He checks and supervises the work of a IVth Category electrician. 
Category V electrician had to attend to all the break downs and rectify them, 
while Category IV electrician had to attend to minor break-downs but In case of 
major break downs, he would report to Category V electrician. The witness says 
that W W. 1 was given the work of a IV Category electrician only in Morgans 
Pit and whenever a IVth Category electrician looks after the Vth Category 
■electrician’s .job during the absence of the latter he was paid acting allowance 
I.ikewife. W.W. 1 was paid acting allowance whenever Vth Category electrician 
absented due to weekly rest or for other reasons. He asserted that W.W. 1 was 
never in the Vllth Category .that at no tme W,W 1 wcikert independently that 
he nevpt attended to the break downs of electric machinery and that there were 
no major break downs wRen^W W. 1 acted in Vth Category electrician’s place. 
It Is in His evidence that When a TVth Category electrician was doing the work of 
a Vth Category electrician during the absence of the latter, they would send for 
higher category electrician to attend to major break downs. He stated that since 
September. 1066 W.W. 1 was In rotation shifts till February, 1968 . W.W. 1 was 
m baick shifts. They required one electrician of Categor.y VI, 3 electricians oi 
Category V and four electricians of Category IV in the Morgans Pit but there 
■were only one electrician of Category VI and four electrielens in Category V and 
one electrician in Category TV. In May, 1968 W W. 1 was transferred to the 
general .shilt and since then he was as.sistlng the Vlth Category electrician in the 
■Work. He would say that W.W. ] was never given the assistance ol a helper when 
he was working in the back shift but wa.=i given the assl-tance of a raazdoor 
depending Jipon the work entrusted to him According to him, there were no 
vacancies in Category V now in the Morgans Pit and seniority, genera) skill and 
regularity were taken into consideration In promoting nersons and experience 
was included in general skill. According to him. some of the electricians had 
passed some examinations and they were promoted and transferred to some other- 
mines He denied knowledge If electricians who had undergone training in the 
I. T I and- had obtained the certiflefifes there, were automatically promoted from 
Categorv IV to Category VII lrre.spectlve of the existence of any vacancies. 
Although he statSd that the name of Posham and Cartegory VH occuring in 
Ex. W 2 were In hjs own handwriting, he admitted that the description Category VII 
mentioned therein was a mistake. It may be noted here only that even W.W. 1 




1412 THE GA/ETl'E OF INDIA: MARCH 20, 1971/PHALGUNA 29. 1892 I^art 11— 


stated that he waa never in Category VII. The witness also stated that W W. I 
was not competent to be piomoted to Vtn Category. 

12 . M.W. 2 is the Mlne^ Manager, Morgans Pit. His evidence is that W W- 1 
wa.s working at Morgans Pit, that he was paid acting allowance wnenever he 
acted in that capacity and Exs. Ml to M a bear the thump Impieasion ol W.W. 1 
and his (Wiiness sj signatuie which show that W-W. 1 was paid acting allowance 
during the period he worked In the place ol Vth Category electrician. Accoiding 
to him, W-W. 1 worked continuously in the place ot Vth Category elecincian for 
about 2 months continuously. Hp denied that W.W. 1 had acted continuously as 
Vth Category electrician lor mbre than six months prior to the implementation 
ol the Wage Board Recommendations. It is also in his evidence that the Morgans 
Pit had become smaller and so some ol the Vth Category electricians had been 
transferred to other mines and there was no vacancy of Vth Category electiiclan 
in the Morgans Pit since the time he took charge He denied that there was any 
vacancy of Vth Category electrician. 

12 .TT Is clear, in tiie light ol the material placed on record, that W.W. 1 was 
working ais Category Iv" electrician prior to the implementation ol the Wage Board 
Recommendations, that he was placed in new Category IV alter the ipiplementatioD 
of the Wage Board Recommendations and was working as such. It is common, 
ground that whenever W.W. 1 worked as Vth Category electrician during the 
absence ol Vth Category electrician In the Morgan,s Pit he wa.s paid acting 
allowance. Exs. Ml to M 3 also show that W.W. 1 was paid acting allowance 
whenever he worked as-Vth Category electrician. While W.W. 1 and WW. 2 
swear that W.W. 1 knew the work ol Vth Category electrician and had worked 
as a Ehilt incharge electrician in the back shift, the Assistant Engineer, Morgana 
Pit, who Ik examined as M.W. 1 swears that W-W, 1 never worked independent!}' 
and that all electricians, who were in the shifts by turns irra'pective ol the 
category were called shllt electricians. He explained that Ex. W 1 applied to all 
electricians working In shifts including IVth Category electricians. However, 
M.W. 2’.s evidence reveals that W-W. 1 worked in the place ol Vth Category 
flPC'tricl.on lor about 2 months continuously- No doubt, M.W. 1 says that in his 
opinion W.W. 1 was not competent to be placed in that category. But it is 
contended lor the workman that W.W. 1 was working as Shift Incharge in tho 
back shift from 19 Q 5 , lhal he had woUied foi six inoiilhs ccntinnously a< Vth 
Cafegcry electrician and for eight months in alternative weeks and that after the 
dispute was raised in regard to his promotion as Vth Category electrician, he 
was transferred in May, 1968 to the general shill where Vlth Category electrician 
and the Charge-hand and the Assistant Engineer would be present and that the 
transfer was made with the object of denying him the promotion ol Vth Category 
and the wages due to the Vth Category electrician. So it is contended that it is 
an act of victimisation on the part ol the Management. But as stated already, 
the Asaistant Engineer denied it- However. Ex. W 4 shows that even as early 
as 7 th February, 1968 the Union had moved In Ihe matter and the Management 
wi/s contending that there was no vacancy ol the post of Vth Category electrician 
and that W W 1 was never given Independent charge as Vth Category electrician 
in the shift. The above facta clearly disclose that W.W. 1 was transferred to the 
general shift on 23 rd May, 1968 , i.e-, only after the Union took up the cause of 
W.W. 1 In respect of hts promotion to a higher entegory and the wages due to be 
paid to him in the’higher category. The Management contends that Morgans Pit 
had become .smaller, that some of the Vth Category electricians themselves had 
been transferred to other mines from that mine and that there were no vacancies 
ot Vth Category electricians in the Morgans Pit, The evidence of M.W- 1 also 
show.s that since he took charge as Assistant Engineer in Morgans Pit about four 
years ago there were no vacancle.s of Vth Category electrician in the Morgans Pit, 
Eh'en MW. 2 has corroborated it- It Is not the plea of the Union that any number 
nf Vth Catepcry eleclrlcinns can be appointed in the Morgans Pit irrespective of 
vacancies, although It is urged that candidates who had passed I. T. I. examinations 
were promoted from Category IV to Category \HI Irrespective of the vacancies 
In the Company and no material is placed to substantiate the above suggestion 
Though it is urged by the Union in their claims statement that W W. 1 was 
transferred from back shift to the general shift only with the object of denying 
him the wages of a higher category, there are no reasonable grounds to come to 
that conclusion. Further, transfer froni^ one shift to another shift is the right of 
the Management and that right cannot be taken away unless it Is proved to b* 
an act of victimisation or unfair' labour practice. That apart, as stated already, 
the evidence of the' Management’s witnesses Is to the effect that the area of 
operation in Morgans Pit has gone down with the result that a number of higher 
category electricians had to be transferred and .sent to other pinceo Their evidence 
Is in no way shaken. There Is also no material placed on record to disprove the 





Sec. 3 (ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: MARCH 20 . 1971 /PHALGUNA 29 . 1892 141J. 


d'jova evideace on behalf of the Management. The Management cannot be directed 
tij transfer W.W- 1 to another shift from the general shilt so as to enable him tc 
get higher wages. 

14 . The evidence of the Assistant Engineer, M.W, 1 , shows that since 23 rd May, 
1968 W W. 1 was doing the work in the general shilt under Vlth C-degory Electrician 
and that he (W.W, 11 was not doing any work independently. As stated already,, 
the Management has let in evidence that W.W.l was paid the acting allowance 
whenever he did the work of a Vth Category electrician and that is also admitted 
by W W 1 - Although W.W. 1 now say.s that even In the general shilt he was 
doing the duties of a Vth Category electrician, there Is no satisfactory proof to 
that effect. Further it is alg.o hard to accept the above evidence. In the circumst¬ 
ances. I find thst the Management of the Singarenl Collieries Company Limited 
was justified in not paying wages of new Category V to Sri K. Posham and that 
his transfer to the general shift from the back shift Is not mala fide. I also find 
that he Is not entif c'd to my relief. I find the issue accordingly. 

Award is passed as mentioned above.

New Delhi, the 15 th March 1971 


S.O. 1170 .—In pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial Disputes Acl 1047 
(14 of 1947 ), the Central Government hereby publishes the following award of 
the Central Government Industrial Tribunal (No. 3 ), Dhanbad, In the Industrial 
dispute between the employers in relation to the management of Kustore Colliery 
of Messrs Raneegunge Coal Association Limited, Post Office Kustore District 
Dhanbad smd their workmen, which was received by the Central Government 
on the 9 th March, 1971 .

AWARD 

1 . The Central Government, being of opinion that an industrial dispute exists 
between the employers In relation to the management of Kustore CoUiery of 
Messrs Raneegunge Coal Association Limited, Post Office Kustore, District Dhanbad 
and their workman, Sri Ram Lakhan Singh, Underground MunshI, by its Order

Overman Ram Pravesh Roy and for this misconduct the chargeshcet Ext. M-i

20. In order to appreciate the evidence correctly it is better tq state the law 
on the subject at one place. v c hw 


21. In Indian Iron and Steel Company Vs. their workmen, reported in 1958(IV 
L.L-J., page 260, the Supreme Court agreed with the four test laid down by thfr 
Labour Appellate Tribunal sev^ years earlier in the Bunckinfiham and Carnati<r 
Mills case, with slight modification the Court observed: 


Undoubtedly, the management of a concern has power to direct its owa 
internal «dmirl.stratlon and discipline; bur the power is not unlimited 
and when dispute arises industrial tribunals have given the power to 
see whether the termination of service of a workman is justified and 
to give proper relief. In cases of dismissal on misconduct the tribunal 
does not, however, act as a Court of appeal and substitute its own 
judgement for that of the management. It will interfere: 


(1) When there is want of good faith 


(2) when there is victimization or unfair labour practice 

(3) when the management has been guilty of a basic error or violation. 

of natural justice, and 


(4) where the finding is completely baseless or perverse. 


22. II is not open to the industrial tribunal in such a case to reappreciate the 
evidence on record or to sit on appeal or on the findings reacted at the domestle 
enquiry. The Industrial Tribunal could not act as a court of appeal in scrutinizing 
the evidences and In reaching Its own conclusion. The employer must have a 
free hand In the industrial management of its own alfalrs- No outside agency 
should impose its will unless the action of the employer is lacking in bonafldes- 
or is manifestly perverse or unfair. 


23. The concerned workman was chargesheeted on the allegation that he 
submitled a false report of reloading of 2 tubs of coal on 20th March, 1969 in the 
name of Kuldlp and his gang with bad motive. The case of the concerred workman 
is that there was actually reloading on that date by Sri Kuldlp and his gang and 
that he has been dismissed by the management because he participated in the 
agitation for the election of local official of the Mazdoor Sangh.

Secretary, Colliery Mazdoor Sangh by several workman Including Sri Ram Lakharr

Sang. Ext W-6 is another letter dated 10th December, 1968 addressed to the

the signatories in that letter demanding election by secret bailot in the CoUlery 
Mazdoor Sangh. Dxt. W-4, W-6 and W-6 are filed by the workman In order to 
show that he took leading part In the agitation for election by secret ballot of 
the Kustore branch of Colliery Mazdoor Sangh.

31. Therefore, in thig case I End that the workman had opportunity of adducing 
his defence witnesses upon which he relied and that the principles of natural 
justice hEwe not been infringed. Under such circumstances the Tribunal would 
not try to examine or to sit as a Court of appeal over the conclusion of the 
Enquirying Officer. 

32. In this view of the case I hold that the dismissal of the concerned workman 
:Srl Ram Lakhan Singh. Underground Munshi by the management with effect from 
18th September. 1969 was justified and the concerned workman is not entitled to 
any relief.

Schedule 

‘‘Whether the management of Khas Bhuranglya Col.y., Post Office Mahuda,
